From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. People tell me they don't see anything wrong, but I just can't shake the feeling that they're staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make your face look more even or to deal with the thoughts about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... before I head back out onto the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror. I mean, I don't really know if rubbing it is actually going to change anything, but... I just feel like I have to do it. It's like I'm trying to smooth it out or fix it somehow. I just want it to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're rubbing your face because you actually see something wrong with it, or because you just feel like you need to do it?\n\nPatient: I definitely see something wrong with it. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I just can't stop thinking about my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It just looks... crooked and messed up to me. It's on my mind all the time. When I'm at work stocking shelves at the warehouse, or even if I go out for salsa night, I feel like everyone is looking right at it and noticing how uneven it is. People tell me they don't see anything wrong, but I just can't shake the feeling that they're staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make your face look more even or to deal with the thoughts about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... before I head back out onto the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror. I'll stand there fixing my cap, trying to get it to look right, and just... rubbing at the exact same spot on my face. I do it so much that sometimes I'm really late getting back to work. Because of that, my picking numbers end up falling behind, and my boss keeps getting on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Um... like I mentioned with work, it's gotten to the point where I've actually been written up at the warehouse because my picking gets delayed so much. And outside of work... it's pretty much stopped me from doing anything. \n\nI've started skipping my soccer games, I don't go to salsa nights anymore, and I even skip out on family gatherings now. I just... I don't want to deal with people looking at me, or someone pulling out a phone and trying to take pictures. It's just easier to stay away from everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're rubbing that spot on your face, are you trying to make it look more symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really know if rubbing it is actually going to change anything, but... I just feel like I have to do it. It's like I'm trying to smooth it out or fix it somehow. I just want it to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're rubbing your face because you actually see something wrong with it, or because you just feel like you need to do it?\n\nPatient: I definitely see something wrong with it. Like I was saying before about being in the bathroom at work... when I'm standing there looking in the mirror before I go back out to the inventory floor, I can clearly see how off it looks. That's why I start fixing my cap and rubbing that exact same spot. \n\nI see it, and I just feel like I have to try and fix it. And then I get stuck doing it, which is why I end up being so late that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on my case again. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I just can't stop thinking about my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It just looks... crooked and messed up to me. It's on my mind all the time. When I'm at work stocking shelves at the warehouse, or even if I go out for salsa night, I feel like everyone is looking right at it and noticing how uneven it is. People tell me they don't see anything wrong, but I just can't shake the feeling that they're staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make your face look more even or to deal with the thoughts about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... before I head back out onto the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror. I'll stand there fixing my cap, trying to get it to look right, and just... rubbing at the exact same spot on my face. I do it so much that sometimes I'm really late getting back to work. Because of that, my picking numbers end up falling behind, and my boss keeps getting on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Um... like I mentioned with work, it's gotten to the point where I've actually been written up at the warehouse because my picking gets delayed so much. And outside of work... it's pretty much stopped me from doing anything. \n\nI've started skipping my soccer games, I don't go to salsa nights anymore, and I even skip out on family gatherings now. I just... I don't want to deal with people looking at me, or someone pulling out a phone and trying to take pictures. It's just easier to stay away from everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're rubbing that spot on your face, are you trying to make it look more symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really know if rubbing it is actually going to change anything, but... I just feel like I have to do it. It's like I'm trying to smooth it out or fix it somehow. I just want it to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're rubbing your face because you actually see something wrong with it, or because you just feel like you need to do it?\n\nPatient: I definitely see something wrong with it. Like I was saying before about being in the bathroom at work... when I'm standing there looking in the mirror before I go back out to the inventory floor, I can clearly see how off it looks. That's why I start fixing my cap and rubbing that exact same spot. \n\nI see it, and I just feel like I have to try and fix it. And then I get stuck doing it, which is why I end up being so late that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on my case again. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to rub your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do it when I'm at home by myself all the time. It's not just about other people seeing it... it's about me knowing it's there. Even if I'm completely alone, I still know my face is uneven, so I still feel like I need to try and fix it. It just bothers me no matter what.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F45.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I just can't stop thinking about my face. Specifically, the left side of it. It just looks... crooked and messed up to me. It's on my mind all the time. When I'm at work stocking shelves at the warehouse, or even if I go out for salsa night, I feel like everyone is looking right at it and noticing how uneven it is. People tell me they don't see anything wrong, but I just can't shake the feeling that they're staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make your face look more even or to deal with the thoughts about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... before I head back out onto the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror. I'll stand there fixing my cap, trying to get it to look right, and just... rubbing at the exact same spot on my face. I do it so much that sometimes I'm really late getting back to work. Because of that, my picking numbers end up falling behind, and my boss keeps getting on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Um... like I mentioned with work, it's gotten to the point where I've actually been written up at the warehouse because my picking gets delayed so much. And outside of work... it's pretty much stopped me from doing anything. \n\nI've started skipping my soccer games, I don't go to salsa nights anymore, and I even skip out on family gatherings now. I just... I don't want to deal with people looking at me, or someone pulling out a phone and trying to take pictures. It's just easier to stay away from everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're rubbing that spot on your face, are you trying to make it look more symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really know if rubbing it is actually going to change anything, but... I just feel like I have to do it. It's like I'm trying to smooth it out or fix it somehow. I just want it to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're rubbing your face because you actually see something wrong with it, or because you just feel like you need to do it?\n\nPatient: I definitely see something wrong with it. Like I was saying before about being in the bathroom at work... when I'm standing there looking in the mirror before I go back out to the inventory floor, I can clearly see how off it looks. That's why I start fixing my cap and rubbing that exact same spot. \n\nI see it, and I just feel like I have to try and fix it. And then I get stuck doing it, which is why I end up being so late that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on my case again. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to rub your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do it when I'm at home by myself all the time. It's not just about other people seeing it... it's about me knowing it's there. Even if I'm completely alone, I still know my face is uneven, so I still feel like I need to try and fix it. It just bothers me no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to rub your face driven more by the idea that it will improve your appearance or by a feeling that you just have to do it?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit, but... it's definitely about trying to fix how it looks. It's not just some random physical urge where my hands just have to do something. I'm doing it because I'm hoping if I rub it enough, it'll actually even out and look normal. So yeah, it's all about trying to improve the appearance.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to rub your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do it when I'm at home by myself all the time. It's not just about other people seeing it... it's about me knowing it's there. Even if I'm completely alone, I still know my face is uneven, so I still feel like I need to try and fix it. It just bothers me no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to rub your face driven more by the idea that it will improve your appearance or by a feeling that you just have to do it?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit, but... it's definitely about trying to fix how it looks. It's not just some random physical urge where my hands just have to do something. I'm doing it because I'm hoping if I rub it enough, it'll actually even out and look normal. So yeah, it's all about trying to improve the appearance.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel like you've successfully fixed or improved the appearance of your face, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I might think for a second that maybe I pushed it back into place a little, but then I look in the mirror again or just feel it, and it's still wrong. It never actually stays fixed. I never get to a point where I feel like, okay, it looks normal now. It's just a constant cycle of trying and failing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Um... before I head back out onto the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror. I'll stand there fixing my cap, trying to get it to look right, and just... rubbing at the exact same spot on my face. I do it so much that sometimes I'm really late getting back to work. Because of that, my picking numbers end up falling behind, and my boss keeps getting on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Um... like I mentioned with work, it's gotten to the point where I've actually been written up at the warehouse because my picking gets delayed so much. And outside of work... it's pretty much stopped me from doing anything. \n\nI've started skipping my soccer games, I don't go to salsa nights anymore, and I even skip out on family gatherings now. I just... I don't want to deal with people looking at me, or someone pulling out a phone and trying to take pictures. It's just easier to stay away from everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're rubbing that spot on your face, are you trying to make it look more symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really know if rubbing it is actually going to change anything, but... I just feel like I have to do it. It's like I'm trying to smooth it out or fix it somehow. I just want it to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're rubbing your face because you actually see something wrong with it, or because you just feel like you need to do it?\n\nPatient: I definitely see something wrong with it. Like I was saying before about being in the bathroom at work... when I'm standing there looking in the mirror before I go back out to the inventory floor, I can clearly see how off it looks. That's why I start fixing my cap and rubbing that exact same spot. \n\nI see it, and I just feel like I have to try and fix it. And then I get stuck doing it, which is why I end up being so late that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on my case again. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to rub your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do it when I'm at home by myself all the time. It's not just about other people seeing it... it's about me knowing it's there. Even if I'm completely alone, I still know my face is uneven, so I still feel like I need to try and fix it. It just bothers me no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to rub your face driven more by the idea that it will improve your appearance or by a feeling that you just have to do it?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit, but... it's definitely about trying to fix how it looks. It's not just some random physical urge where my hands just have to do something. I'm doing it because I'm hoping if I rub it enough, it'll actually even out and look normal. So yeah, it's all about trying to improve the appearance.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel like you've successfully fixed or improved the appearance of your face, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I might think for a second that maybe I pushed it back into place a little, but then I look in the mirror again or just feel it, and it's still wrong. It never actually stays fixed. I never get to a point where I feel like, okay, it looks normal now. It's just a constant cycle of trying and failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you were convinced that it looked perfectly even and normal?\n\nPatient: I mean... if I actually believed it looked normal? No, I don't think I would. Like I said, the whole reason I do it is to try and fix the unevenness. If there was nothing wrong with it, I wouldn't have any reason to rub it. It's just... hard to even imagine that, because I know how bad it looks.\n\nDoctor: Is the act of rubbing your face something that you feel is necessary to do, regardless of whether or not it actually improves your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I don't feel a need to just do it for the sake of doing it. Like I was saying before, it's always tied to trying to fix how uneven my face is. I don't have this urge to just rub it regardless of that. It's always, always about trying to make it look right, even if it doesn't end up working.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

And outside of work... it's pretty much stopped me from doing anything. \n\nI've started skipping my soccer games, I don't go to salsa nights anymore, and I even skip out on family gatherings now. I just... I don't want to deal with people looking at me, or someone pulling out a phone and trying to take pictures. It's just easier to stay away from everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're rubbing that spot on your face, are you trying to make it look more symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really know if rubbing it is actually going to change anything, but... I just feel like I have to do it. It's like I'm trying to smooth it out or fix it somehow. I just want it to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're rubbing your face because you actually see something wrong with it, or because you just feel like you need to do it?\n\nPatient: I definitely see something wrong with it. Like I was saying before about being in the bathroom at work... when I'm standing there looking in the mirror before I go back out to the inventory floor, I can clearly see how off it looks. That's why I start fixing my cap and rubbing that exact same spot. \n\nI see it, and I just feel like I have to try and fix it. And then I get stuck doing it, which is why I end up being so late that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on my case again. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to rub your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do it when I'm at home by myself all the time. It's not just about other people seeing it... it's about me knowing it's there. Even if I'm completely alone, I still know my face is uneven, so I still feel like I need to try and fix it. It just bothers me no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to rub your face driven more by the idea that it will improve your appearance or by a feeling that you just have to do it?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit, but... it's definitely about trying to fix how it looks. It's not just some random physical urge where my hands just have to do something. I'm doing it because I'm hoping if I rub it enough, it'll actually even out and look normal. So yeah, it's all about trying to improve the appearance.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel like you've successfully fixed or improved the appearance of your face, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I might think for a second that maybe I pushed it back into place a little, but then I look in the mirror again or just feel it, and it's still wrong. It never actually stays fixed. I never get to a point where I feel like, okay, it looks normal now. It's just a constant cycle of trying and failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you were convinced that it looked perfectly even and normal?\n\nPatient: I mean... if I actually believed it looked normal? No, I don't think I would. Like I said, the whole reason I do it is to try and fix the unevenness. If there was nothing wrong with it, I wouldn't have any reason to rub it. It's just... hard to even imagine that, because I know how bad it looks.\n\nDoctor: Is the act of rubbing your face something that you feel is necessary to do, regardless of whether or not it actually improves your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I don't feel a need to just do it for the sake of doing it. Like I was saying before, it's always tied to trying to fix how uneven my face is. I don't have this urge to just rub it regardless of that. It's always, always about trying to make it look right, even if it doesn't end up working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look uneven to you if a plastic surgeon or multiple people told you it was perfectly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, I think it would. I mean... people already tell me they don't see it, and it doesn't change anything for me. That's kind of why I'm here. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and totally messed up. Like, the guys at the warehouse or people at salsa night will tell me they don't see anything wrong when I ask them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing it. So, yeah, even if a professional told me it was perfectly symmetrical, I'd probably still see it as crooked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People tell me they don't see anything wrong, but I just can't shake the feeling that they're staring at it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make your face look more even or to deal with the thoughts about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Um... before I head back out onto the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in the bathroom mirror. I'll stand there fixing my cap, trying to get it to look right, and just... rubbing at the exact same spot on my face. I do it so much that sometimes I'm really late getting back to work. Because of that, my picking numbers end up falling behind, and my boss keeps getting on me about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about your face are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely is. Um... like I mentioned with work, it's gotten to the point where I've actually been written up at the warehouse because my picking gets delayed so much. And outside of work... it's pretty much stopped me from doing anything. \n\nI've started skipping my soccer games, I don't go to salsa nights anymore, and I even skip out on family gatherings now. I just... I don't want to deal with people looking at me, or someone pulling out a phone and trying to take pictures. It's just easier to stay away from everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're rubbing that spot on your face, are you trying to make it look more symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. I mean, I don't really know if rubbing it is actually going to change anything, but... I just feel like I have to do it. It's like I'm trying to smooth it out or fix it somehow. I just want it to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're rubbing your face because you actually see something wrong with it, or because you just feel like you need to do it?\n\nPatient: I definitely see something wrong with it. Like I was saying before about being in the bathroom at work... when I'm standing there looking in the mirror before I go back out to the inventory floor, I can clearly see how off it looks. That's why I start fixing my cap and rubbing that exact same spot. \n\nI see it, and I just feel like I have to try and fix it. And then I get stuck doing it, which is why I end up being so late that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on my case again. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to rub your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do it when I'm at home by myself all the time. It's not just about other people seeing it... it's about me knowing it's there. Even if I'm completely alone, I still know my face is uneven, so I still feel like I need to try and fix it. It just bothers me no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to rub your face driven more by the idea that it will improve your appearance or by a feeling that you just have to do it?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit, but... it's definitely about trying to fix how it looks. It's not just some random physical urge where my hands just have to do something. I'm doing it because I'm hoping if I rub it enough, it'll actually even out and look normal. So yeah, it's all about trying to improve the appearance.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel like you've successfully fixed or improved the appearance of your face, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I might think for a second that maybe I pushed it back into place a little, but then I look in the mirror again or just feel it, and it's still wrong. It never actually stays fixed. I never get to a point where I feel like, okay, it looks normal now. It's just a constant cycle of trying and failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you were convinced that it looked perfectly even and normal?\n\nPatient: I mean... if I actually believed it looked normal? No, I don't think I would. Like I said, the whole reason I do it is to try and fix the unevenness. If there was nothing wrong with it, I wouldn't have any reason to rub it. It's just... hard to even imagine that, because I know how bad it looks.\n\nDoctor: Is the act of rubbing your face something that you feel is necessary to do, regardless of whether or not it actually improves your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I don't feel a need to just do it for the sake of doing it. Like I was saying before, it's always tied to trying to fix how uneven my face is. I don't have this urge to just rub it regardless of that. It's always, always about trying to make it look right, even if it doesn't end up working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look uneven to you if a plastic surgeon or multiple people told you it was perfectly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, I think it would. I mean... people already tell me they don't see it, and it doesn't change anything for me. That's kind of why I'm here. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and totally messed up. Like, the guys at the warehouse or people at salsa night will tell me they don't see anything wrong when I ask them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing it. So, yeah, even if a professional told me it was perfectly symmetrical, I'd probably still see it as crooked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face might actually be symmetrical, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess logically there's a possibility, since other people keep telling me it's fine. But I just can't see it that way at all. Like I was just saying, that's the whole reason I'm here. I literally can't stop thinking about how crooked and messed up the left side of my face looks. \n\nEven when the guys at the warehouse or the people at salsa night tell me they don't see anything, I still feel like everyone is staring at it and noticing it. It just constantly plays on my mind, so it's really, really hard for me to believe it might actually be normal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

And then I get stuck doing it, which is why I end up being so late that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on my case again. But yeah, it definitely starts because I see something wrong right there in the mirror.\n\nDoctor: Is the urge to rub your face something that you feel you can control, or does it feel like something you have to do regardless of what you want?\n\nPatient: It really feels like something I have to do. I don't feel like I have control over it at all. Um, like I was just saying... before I go back on the inventory floor, I just keep checking my face in that bathroom mirror. I'll be fixing my cap and rubbing at the exact same spot, and I just can't stop myself and walk away. \n\nI get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm so late from doing it that my picking numbers fall behind and my boss gets on me again. If I could just control it and stop, I wouldn't be risking my job, you know? But I just... I feel like I have to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror?\n\nPatient: I don't really know. I mean, I guess I've never really been in a situation where I couldn't see myself at all for a long time. But... even if I'm not looking in a mirror, I still feel it. Like, I know it's there. So I'd probably still rub it, just because I can feel how wrong it is, even if I'm not looking right at it. But the mirror definitely makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the rubbing would stop if your concern about the unevenness of your face was addressed?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure. I'd like to think that if it was fixed—if my face didn't look so uneven—then I wouldn't need to rub it anymore. Because there wouldn't be anything to try and, you know, smooth out. \n\nBut... honestly, I don't know for a fact if it would just stop. I've been dealing with this for so long that I haven't really been in a situation where I didn't worry about it. So I guess I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious or uncomfortable if you couldn't rub your face when you feel the need to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. If I couldn't rub it, I'd get really anxious. It's like this pressure builds up, and I just have to do it. If something stopped me from being able to touch my face, I think I'd probably panic, to be honest. It would be really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face is because you're worried about how you look or because you just feel like you need to rub it?\n\nPatient: I think... it's mostly because I'm worried about how I look. Like, the whole reason I feel the need to rub it in the first place is because I know it's uneven and I'm trying to fix it. So if I can't rub it, I just sit there thinking about how bad it looks and how everyone can probably see it. I guess the urge to rub it is just tied directly to worrying about my face.\n\nDoctor: Is the anxiety you feel when you can't rub your face more about the sensation of not rubbing or the thought of your face being uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the thought of it being uneven. It's not really about my hands needing to move or missing the physical feeling of doing it. It's just... if I'm not doing it, my mind is completely stuck on how lopsided my face is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to rub your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I do it when I'm at home by myself all the time. It's not just about other people seeing it... it's about me knowing it's there. Even if I'm completely alone, I still know my face is uneven, so I still feel like I need to try and fix it. It just bothers me no matter what.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling that you need to rub your face driven more by the idea that it will improve your appearance or by a feeling that you just have to do it?\n\nPatient: I mean, I feel like I'm repeating myself a bit, but... it's definitely about trying to fix how it looks. It's not just some random physical urge where my hands just have to do something. I'm doing it because I'm hoping if I rub it enough, it'll actually even out and look normal. So yeah, it's all about trying to improve the appearance.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel like you've successfully fixed or improved the appearance of your face, even if it's just for a short while?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I might think for a second that maybe I pushed it back into place a little, but then I look in the mirror again or just feel it, and it's still wrong. It never actually stays fixed. I never get to a point where I feel like, okay, it looks normal now. It's just a constant cycle of trying and failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you were convinced that it looked perfectly even and normal?\n\nPatient: I mean... if I actually believed it looked normal? No, I don't think I would. Like I said, the whole reason I do it is to try and fix the unevenness. If there was nothing wrong with it, I wouldn't have any reason to rub it. It's just... hard to even imagine that, because I know how bad it looks.\n\nDoctor: Is the act of rubbing your face something that you feel is necessary to do, regardless of whether or not it actually improves your appearance?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean... I don't feel a need to just do it for the sake of doing it. Like I was saying before, it's always tied to trying to fix how uneven my face is. I don't have this urge to just rub it regardless of that. It's always, always about trying to make it look right, even if it doesn't end up working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look uneven to you if a plastic surgeon or multiple people told you it was perfectly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, I think it would. I mean... people already tell me they don't see it, and it doesn't change anything for me. That's kind of why I'm here. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and totally messed up. Like, the guys at the warehouse or people at salsa night will tell me they don't see anything wrong when I ask them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing it. I never get to a point where I feel like, okay, it looks normal now. It's just a constant cycle of trying and failing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to rub your face if you were convinced that it looked perfectly even and normal?\n\nPatient: I mean... if I actually believed it looked normal? No, I don't think I would. Like I said, the whole reason I do it is to try and fix the unevenness. If there was nothing wrong with it, I wouldn't have any reason to rub it. I don't have this urge to just rub it regardless of that. It's always, always about trying to make it look right, even if it doesn't end up working.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would still look uneven to you if a plastic surgeon or multiple people told you it was perfectly symmetrical?\n\nPatient: Yeah, honestly, I think it would. I just can't stop thinking about how the left side of my face looks crooked and totally messed up. Like, the guys at the warehouse or people at salsa night will tell me they don't see anything wrong when I ask them, but I still feel like everyone is noticing it. So, yeah, even if a professional told me it was perfectly symmetrical, I'd probably still see it as crooked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your face might actually be symmetrical, but you just can't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess logically there's a possibility, since other people keep telling me it's fine. But I just can't see it that way at all. Like I was just saying, that's the whole reason I'm here. It just constantly plays on my mind, so it's really, really hard for me to believe it might actually be normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that your perception of your face being uneven is not based on how it actually looks?\n\nPatient: I mean, I guess?
